# Decisions: agent integration layer

Why trajectories and observed events are normalized the way they are, and why agent-specific knowledge lives in `pkg/agent` adapters rather than in the core. D1 to D14 keep the numbering of the original integration design; entries added during implementation are numbered from I-15.

Entries carry their own dates. Statuses reconciled with the code at commit d223e7d on 2026-10-28. Original source: docs/archive/07_trajectory_formats.md (local only). Related: [verification.md](verification.md), [open.md](open.md), [../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md](../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md).

Evidence from the maintainers' own Claude Code and Gemini sessions was used as design input only. It has no paired ground truth and is not an evaluation set, so no figure from it appears here.

## Core decisions (agent-independent)

### D1: The matching surface is per tool, not global
Status: Accepted, implemented in the adapters' tool maps (`pkg/agent/claudecode`, `pkg/agent/gemini`).
Date: 2026-09 (design), 2026-10-06 (built).
Context: A trajectory claims at tool granularity, the probes record syscalls. A single correspondence rule produces false verdicts in both directions.
Decision: A shell-command claim is verified at level 1 (the process the agent spawned). A file-tool claim is verified at level 0, against the agent process, with content where the format reports it. The adapter declares the mapping and the core applies it.
Consequences: The core holds no tool names. Adding a tool is an adapter change.
Alternatives rejected: One global rule for every claim, which cannot express both a direct file write and a command that writes through descendants.

### D2: A shell-command entry is a container
Status: Accepted, implemented in `pkg/verification.Forest.Attribute` and `CheckCoverage`. Amended 2026-10-06 (commit b1709a8): wording corrected when the entry moved here from the original design; the parenthesis in Decision says what changed.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits 4f83cfb, 2130154).
Context: Compound shell commands are common, so one claim routinely corresponds to several executions plus their file and network activity (measured on a local transcript corpus, design input only (local only: docs/research/harness_corpus.md)).
Decision: A shell-command entry matches on its recovered command string and owns every event in the subtree of its level-1 process. Ownership is by process ancestry, not by a time window. (The original wording, "owns every descendant event in its window", predates the forest model and is corrected here; D4 and V1 already required ancestry.)
Consequences: A command that runs for hours, or is approved after a long pause, owns its events the same way as a fast one.
Alternatives rejected: Expanding a compound command into one entry per program. It invents claims the agent never made and weakens the omission and fabrication arguments.

### D3: Level 2 and below are never matched against trajectory entries
Status: Accepted, implemented in `Forest.Partition` (descendant events are content of a command, never in the aligned sequence).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit a147518).
Context: The agent did not claim what its commands' descendants did.
Decision: Descendant events can be neither corroborated nor unrecorded. They are attributed and counted toward coverage.
Consequences: A request made by a forked `curl` is attributed to the command's subtree and cannot be an end-to-end claim (so a request-hash substitution is tested at unit level, not by an end-to-end test with a forked `curl`).
Alternatives rejected: Aligning descendants against claims, which would require claims nobody writes.

### D4: Level-2 ancestry is load-bearing for the verdict, negatively
Status: Accepted, implemented in `BuildForest` and `CheckCoverage` (V3).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits 4f83cfb, 2130154).
Context: A `net_connect` or `file_write` inside a claimed command needs no claim of its own. Without ancestry such events are orphans and an honest trajectory reads as NOT FAITHFUL.
Decision: Attribution is by ancestry, never by time. Whatever suppresses an event from the unclaimed set is what an attacker attacks: suppression by lineage needs a real causal link, suppression by a time window only needs coincidence, and the claim interval is adversary-controlled.
Consequences: Descendant `process_exec` records may be dropped under volume pressure only if the PID-to-command ancestry survives. Lost tree records are counted as event loss (P-10 in [probes.md](probes.md)).
Alternatives rejected: Time-window attribution (see F0.2-F0.3 in [verification.md](verification.md)).

### D5: Level 2 is also the capability record, judged in a separate layer
Status: Accepted, implemented as `Verdict.Capability` and the per-command content in `Partition`.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits ec68569, a147518).
Context: Whether `make test` should have opened a socket has no counterpart in the trajectory, so matching cannot decide it.
Decision: Capture and attribute level-2 events, and keep their judgment out of the faithfulness verdict.
Consequences: Laundering through a script (an agent writes `helper.sh` and runs it) stays FAITHFUL, correctly, but becomes visible as a subtree with content. FAITHFUL is not integrity, and reports must say so.
Alternatives rejected: Discarding descendant events, which the first engine did.

### D6: The observed argv is not the claimed command
Status: Accepted, implemented in `claudecode.Adapter.Normalize` (`evalPayload`). Gemini's `Normalize` is the identity because its wrapping is unmeasured.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit 7d52eea).
Context: Claude Code runs each Bash call through a wrapper that sources a shell snapshot, adjusts options and ends with `eval '<command>'` plus a `pwd -P` write.
Decision: `Normalize` extracts the `eval` payload and undoes shell quoting with a small POSIX word parser. A line that is not a wrapper, or cannot be parsed, is left alone so it shows as a mismatch instead of being guessed at. The wrapper is versioned inside the adapter, never in `pkg/matching`.
Consequences: A wrapper change in a new harness version surfaces as substitutions on every Bash claim, which is loud rather than silent.
Alternatives rejected: Loosening target comparison in the core.

### D7: Never assume a level-1 exec exists
Status: Accepted. Partly implemented: Gemini `RunPersistent` commands are not claimed (I-17). Degraded verification for persistent shells is open (O-11 in [open.md](open.md)).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits 53d4f48, 4876c5b).
Context: An agent with a persistent shell writes commands to stdin and produces no per-call exec. Gemini's `run_command` carries a `RunPersistent` flag.
Decision: The adapter declares the process model (`ProcessModel.ShellPerCommand`) instead of the core assuming one.
Consequences: Without a level-1 child, both coverage and alignment lose their subject for that claim.
Alternatives rejected: Assuming one exec per shell claim for every agent.

### D8: Parsing a Claude Code session means merging its `subagents/` directory
Status: Accepted, implemented in `claudecode.Adapter.Parse`.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit 7d52eea).
Context: Subagent turns are never inline in the main transcript. They live in `<session>/subagents/agent-<id>.jsonl`, share the session id, and interleave by timestamp.
Decision: Parse merges the subagent files into the main claim sequence.
Consequences: A session read without its directory misses claims and reads as omissions.
Alternatives rejected: Reading only the main transcript.

### D9: Flatten subagents, keeping the originating thread id as metadata
Status: Accepted, implemented (`TrajectoryEntry.ThreadID`). The alignment does not yet use it (O-29); `BlockID` is used since V-32.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits 56fef6e, 7d52eea).
Context: A `/proc` ancestry check on a live Claude Code session showed that a subagent's shell commands are children of the same agent PID as the main thread's. Nothing in the observed data distinguishes a subagent call.
Decision: Flatten subagent claims into one sequence and keep the thread id on each entry. Forced by the observation, not chosen.
Consequences: Concurrent subagents make the claim order partial (V2). The thread id comes from the trajectory, which the attacker controls.
Alternatives rejected: Nesting subagent claims under the `Agent` call, which has nothing in G to hang on.

### D10: Non-effectful tools must not become entries
Status: Accepted, implemented in each adapter's `nonEffectful` set and `Report`.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commits 53d4f48, 7d52eea).
Context: Control-flow and UI tools perform no syscall. Emitting them produces permanent Unwitnessed entries, a false fabrication signal.
Decision: The adapter supplies the allowlist. The core requires that unmapped tools be counted, never silently dropped: a tool that is neither mapped nor declared non-effectful is named in `Report.UnknownTools` and in `Degradations`.
Consequences: An unknown tool that did act shows up as an unexplained event plus a named degradation, not as a silent pass.
Alternatives rejected: Dropping unknown tools silently, or treating them as non-effectful by default.

### D11: The harness generates its own unclaimed events, which must be filtered
Status: Accepted. Implemented as a measured baseline (V7, `agent.Baseline`, `cmd/baseline`); see I-19.
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit 5b551d7).
Context: Measured on Claude Code: persistent connections from the agent process (model API and telemetry) and one file read plus one file write per shell call from the wrapper. Left unfiltered, no real trajectory can be FAITHFUL.
Decision: Every adapter's harness noise is removed before coverage is evaluated. The membership is agent-specific and established by measurement, not by hand.
Consequences: Verification without a baseline for the agent version reports a degradation.
Alternatives rejected: A hardcoded list of addresses and globs, which rots silently into false omissions when an endpoint moves.

### D12: The claim interval comes from the format, never from configuration
Status: Accepted, implemented (`TrajectoryEntry.Timestamp` and `End`).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit 56fef6e).
Context: Both surveyed formats bracket execution: Claude Code pairs `tool_use` with `tool_result`, Gemini records created, started and completed.
Decision: An entry carries the interval the trajectory supplies. Where the format has no end, the entry is a point and the adapter records the degradation rather than synthesizing a width.
Consequences: The interval is adversary-controlled, so it is used only as a consistency finding (V-12), never to attribute or pair.
Alternatives rejected: A configured tolerance window.

### D13: A reported start is a decision time, not an execution time, unless the format says otherwise
Status: Accepted, implemented as `ProcessModel.IntervalKind` (`IntervalDecision` for Claude Code, `IntervalExecution` for Gemini).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit 53d4f48).
Context: Claude Code stamps `tool_use` when the model emits the call. The permission prompt comes after, so human approval latency sits inside the interval. Call durations have a long tail dominated by approval waits (measured on a local transcript corpus, design input only (local only: docs/research/harness_corpus.md)).
Decision: An adapter declares what its start timestamp means. Gemini's `started` field is a real execution anchor.
Consequences: Claude Code intervals cannot bound execution. Gemini intervals can.
Alternatives rejected: Treating every start as execution time.

### D14: Entry-to-exec pairing is an order-preserving assignment
Status: Accepted, implemented by the per-lane alignment (V4, V-9).
Date: design 2026-09 (in the archived integration design, before 2026-09-19); built 2026-10-06 (commit a147518).
Context: A nearest-in-time lookup fails under approval latency and long commands, and mispairs repeated identical commands under clock skew.
Decision: For a sequential agent the nth shell claim corresponds to the nth level-1 child. Adapters must preserve claim order and expose what the format knows about concurrency (block and thread ids).
Consequences: The clock is used only for relative order, so a constant offset between the agent's clock and the kernel's is harmless.
Alternatives rejected: Nearest-in-time lookup.

## Decisions taken while building the adapters

### I-15: Exit codes are not claimed by either real adapter
Status: Accepted, implemented in `claudecode.Adapter.Process` and `gemini.Adapter.Process` (`ExitsClaimed: false`), applied by `agent.Prepare` as `Options.IgnoreExits`. Listed as open in the plan documents as O-4, which the user closed on 2026-10-11 by ratifying this entry.
Date: 2026-10-06 (commits 7d52eea, 4876c5b).
Context: Claude Code reports only `is_error`. Gemini records nothing. `is_error` is also set for harness failures (timeout, denied permission, malformed input), so it conflates "the command failed" with "the command never ran".
Decision: `ExitCode` stays nil for Claude Code and Gemini. `Expresses` returns false for `exit_code`, and process exits are dropped from both sides before alignment. `Generic` and `simagent` keep exits on.
Consequences: Real-agent verdicts never check exit status. The three-valued nil (V-20) makes this cost nothing in false findings.
Alternatives rejected: A two-valued exit claim derived from `is_error`, which invents a value the format does not hold.

### I-16: Claude Code `WebSearch` is non-effectful; Gemini `search_web` is unknown
Status: Accepted, implemented in the adapters' `nonEffectful` sets. Amended by V-45 (2026-10-26): a Claude Code `WebSearch` call is also reported as a server-side call, joined to the capture's responses. Amended by I-72 (2026-10-27): a Claude Code `WebFetch` of 2.1.306 also claims its request and the harness's domain check.
Date: 2026-10-02 (the traced run in Context), built 2026-10-06.
Context: A traced run of Claude Code 2.1.286 (strace on `connect`, `sendto`, `sendmsg`, `write`, one run, Verified) showed `WebSearch` opens no connection to any search or content host, only to the model API and telemetry. The same method confirmed `WebFetch` connects directly from the agent process. Whether Gemini's `search_web` runs server-side is unverified.
Decision: `WebSearch` is in Claude Code's non-effectful set, so it produces no entry. Gemini's `search_web` is neither mapped nor declared non-effectful, so it is named in `UnknownTools` and `Degradations`.
Consequences: A Claude Code web search is not verified by this system; verifying it needs the model API stream (O-14). A false non-effectful declaration would hide a real client-side action, which is why Gemini's stays unknown.
Alternatives rejected: Mapping `WebSearch` to a `net_connect` claim, which would be a permanent false fabrication.

### I-17: Gemini steps that did not complete, and persistent-shell commands, are not claimed
Status: Accepted, implemented in `gemini.Adapter.Parse` (`statusCompleted`, `reasonPersistent`). Amended 2026-11-05 by I-134: a `run_command` canceled after it moved to the background is claimed.
Date: 2026-10-06 (commit 4876c5b).
Context: A step whose status is not completed may never have executed. A `RunPersistent` command produces no level-1 exec (D7).
Decision: Neither becomes a claim. Both are counted by name with the reason, and non-completed steps add a degradation.
Consequences: A claim nothing could witness is not reported as a false fabrication. The actions of a persistent shell, if any, appear unexplained.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming every step, which turns harness bookkeeping into Unwitnessed findings.

### I-18: Claim order in the fs lane follows the kernel's order
Status: Superseded (Claude Code part) by I-22, (Gemini part) by I-50. Accepted for `simagent`. Amended 2026-10-07 (commit d124fc9): the original status was "Accepted, implemented in `simagent` and the adapters' file-tool claim shapes. The shapes for real agents are a hypothesis (O-16)."
Date: 2026-10-06 (commits da6ccc8, 81b3c4d).
Context: The alignment pairs by position within a lane, so claim order must match the order the probe reports. fanotify reports a new file as create (a directory record covering the file, `PathIsAmbiguous`), open, modify, close, and merges consecutive events on one object.
Decision: Adapters emit fs claims in the kernel's order, and merged events sit where the kernel queued them. On the observed side a merged mask expands as open, write, close (P-13 in [probes.md](probes.md)).
Consequences: Current shapes: Claude Code `Read` is `file_open`, `Write` and `Edit` are open, write, close; Gemini's file tools follow the same pattern. Neither adapter claims the create record, and fanotify merging can change how many events a burst produces, so a claim cannot assume a multiplicity.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming in the order the tool describes its work, which read honest runs as fabrications.

### I-19: `IsHarnessNoise` declares nothing in the real adapters
Status: Accepted, implemented (`IsHarnessNoise` returns false in `claudecode` and `gemini`).
Date: 2026-10-06.
Context: The harness's own activity changes by version and endpoint.
Decision: Harness activity comes only from the measured baseline (V7, V-19). The adapters declare no static noise.
Consequences: A static filter cannot rot into false omissions, and cannot match a path an attacker writes to. Until a baseline is captured, harness activity reads as omissions.
Alternatives rejected: The address and glob list observed on one version.

### I-20: Expressibility is declared per tool
Status: Accepted; Context partly outdated by I-31 (an Edit now claims hashes when its result record has the fields). Implemented as `Adapter.Expresses` and `TrajectoryEntry.Tool`.
Date: 2026-10-06 (commits 53d4f48, 7d52eea).
Context: On Claude Code, `Edit` carries fragments (nil output hash by format) while `Write` carries full content (nil by choice), in the same field on the same agent.
Decision: Each adapter answers, per entry and field, whether its format can state the field. `TrajectoryEntry.Tool` carries the tool name as provenance for that answer. Claude Code states an output hash only for `Write`, Gemini only for `write_to_file`; neither states an exit code or a request body.
Consequences: The core's three-valued nil (V-20) gets its answer from the adapter.
Alternatives rejected: A per-agent or per-field declaration, which cannot separate `Edit` from `Write`.

### I-21: Adapter detection refuses to guess
Status: Accepted, implemented in `agent.Detect` and `cmd/verify --agent`.
Date: 2026-10-06 (commit 5b551d7).
Context: `cmd/verify` selects an adapter from the trajectory file when `--agent` is not given.
Decision: `Detect` returns an error when no adapter or several recognize the file. `cmd/verify` falls back to `Generic` only when nothing recognizes it.
Consequences: A misdetection cannot silently change which tool map and noise rules apply.
Alternatives rejected: First-match detection by registration order.

### I-22: Claude Code file tools are claimed at the grain of the tool call, and the observed side is rewritten to match
Status: Accepted; the Edit-without-hash part superseded by I-31; a Read's open carries the content it read since I-40. Implemented (`claudecode.Adapter.NormalizeStream` in `stream.go`, `agent.StreamNormalizer`). Supersedes the Claude Code part of I-18.
Date: 2026-10-07 (paired capture of Claude Code 2.1.286, **Verified** on one task, one machine).
Context: A paired capture showed that `Read` opens the file once, and that `Write` and `Edit` replace a file atomically through a temporary named `<path>.tmp.<pid>.<12 hex>`. fanotify reports a replace as a create on the directory, then open, write and close of the temporary, then a rename on the directory, with the close carrying the hash of the final content. The close can be read after the rename, in which case it names the target. `Edit`, and a `Write` over an existing file, first open the target one or more times. The trajectory records one call per tool.
Decision: One claim per tool call where the call is one action: `Read` is a `file_open`, `Write` a `file_write` carrying the content hash (preceded by a `file_open` unless the harness's own `toolUseResult` says the file was created), `Edit` a `file_open` then a `file_write` with no hash. The observed side is rewritten to the same grain by an optional `StreamNormalizer` that `agent.Prepare` runs after per-event `Normalize`: a finished temp-and-rename, in either order of close and rename, becomes one `file_write` of the target with the close's hash, and a run of opens of one file by one process becomes one open. The claims collapse adjacent opens of one file the same way, so the two sides stay comparable.
Consequences: An honest session verifies, and an unclaimed atomic write is still an omission. A temporary that is never closed and renamed, a name that does not match the harness's pattern, and events of other processes are left alone, so they show as unexplained. A write that does not use a temporary (not seen) will show as unexplained, and the report says so. Collapsing opens loses the count of repeated reads of one file.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming each of the five kernel events (the temporary's name is random, and the reads before a replace vary in number); making the verifier ignore multiplicity (it would lose the ability to count claims against events elsewhere); dropping the harness's pre-reads outright (that would also drop the open a separate `Read` claim needs).

### I-23: Per-run ids in the harness's own commands are replaced on the observed side
Status: Accepted, implemented (`canonicalIDs` in `claudecode/shell.go`).
Date: 2026-10-07.
Context: The commands the harness runs the first time a session uses Bash name a snapshot file and a heredoc delimiter by ids that differ every run, and the wrapper names a working-directory file the same way. The ids are base 36, not hex. Two runs of the same behaviour would never match a baseline rule.
Decision: `Normalize` replaces the three ids with fixed tokens in process targets that are not recovered commands. A claim never names these, so only the observed side is rewritten.
Consequences: A baseline rule for a setup command is stable across runs. File targets are not rewritten.
Alternatives rejected: Pattern rules in the baseline (a wildcard explains whatever an attacker makes it match, V-19).

### I-24: A baseline names the watched directory with a placeholder and takes a measured agreement threshold
Status: Accepted, implemented (`agent.WorkspacePlaceholder`, `Baseline.Predicate(workspace)`, `CaptureOptions.MinAgreement`, `models.GroundTruthFile.Workspace`). Amends V-19 (`--min-agreement`).
Date: 2026-10-07.
Context: The harness lists and inspects the working directory, so its commands name it, and the shell snapshot command it runs at the first Bash call differs with the enabled tool set (it shadows find and grep only when Grep and Glob are disabled); a baseline measured in one directory would otherwise not apply in another. Some harness activity is occasional: a probe for package managers appeared in two of three control runs.
Decision: Capture rewrites the run's workspace (recorded by `watch`) to `{workspace}` in rule targets, and the predicate expands it to the workspace of the run being verified. A rule still matches an action type and target exactly. `--min-agreement` sets the fraction of control runs that must perform an action for it to become a rule, one by default.
Consequences: A baseline is reusable across directories, and holds for one agent version and one tool set (the capture script runs the controls with the task's tools and refuses a mismatched reuse). At full agreement an occasional action is left out and can read as unexplained in a later run, and lowering the threshold widens the baseline, which is a choice the person makes and the file records (`min_agreement`).
Alternatives rejected: Controls that all run in one directory (the baseline would fit only that directory); a union of every action seen (a wider baseline by default).
Note 2026-10-11: V-24 adopts a 1/N threshold in the capture script (`MIN_AGREEMENT`, default one over the number of controls), while the `cmd/baseline --min-agreement` default stays 1. The two are consistent: the CLI default keeps what every run did, the script default what any one run did. The script makes that choice explicitly and the baseline file records it (`min_agreement`), so the rejection above still holds for the CLI default.

### I-25: Claude Code Grep and Glob are claimed as the kind of search
Status: Accepted; narrowed by I-27 (only the native installer's path is recognised). Implemented (`searchKind`, `SearchGrep`, `SearchGlob` in `claudecode/shell.go`). Extended by I-69 (2026-10-27): a Grep that names a path, on 2.1.296 or 2.1.306, also implies an optional open of the path. Amended 2026-11-02 by I-108: the claim and the observed search name the folder searched.
Date: 2026-10-07 (paired capture of Claude Code 2.1.286, **Verified** on one task).
Context: Grep and Glob run as ripgrep embedded in Claude Code's own binary, started as a level-1 child of the agent whose first argument is `--no-config`. Left unmapped, and so named as unknown in the report (option (c) of O-9 in [open.md](open.md)), each search was an unexplained command. The pattern is not recoverable from the arguments in general, and what a search reads is not something a claim is verified against.
Decision: A Grep or Glob call is a `process_exec` claim with the fixed target `claude-code search:grep` or `claude-code search:glob`. `Normalize` rewrites an observed command line to the same target when its program is `<dir>/claude/versions/<version>` and its first argument is `--no-config`: with `--files` and `--null` it is a Glob, without `--files` a Grep. The harness's own start-up listings (`--files` without `--null`) keep their exact text so a baseline names them.
Consequences: A search that happened is verified to have been claimed, in order, and an unclaimed search is an omission. The pattern and path are not verified. Only the harness's own binary path is recognised, so another program that takes `--no-config` is not rewritten. A search whose arguments change shape in another version can be misclassified (a Glob without `--null`), which shows as a mismatch.
Alternatives rejected: Parsing ripgrep's arguments for the pattern (fragile, and low value); declaring the tools non-effectful (a search is an observable process).

### I-26: The wrapper is recovered only when the whole command line is the wrapper
Status: Accepted, implemented (`evalPayload`, `wrapperHead`, `wrapperTail` in `claudecode/shell.go`). Amended 2026-10-11 (commit 7d266c9), see Decision.
Date: 2026-10-08 (a security review of the adapter, local only).
Context: `evalPayload` recognised a wrapper by the snapshot marker and the first `&& eval `, returned the eval word and discarded the rest of the line. A command appended after the suffix, one run before the `source`, a second word after the eval, or another program whose arguments contained the marker all normalized to the claimed command, so a harness could run arbitrary code under an honest-looking Bash claim, with the subtree explained by the claim (**Verified**: `TestPairedWrapperWithAppendedCommandIsNotFaithful` failed before the change).
Decision: The observed line must match the wrapper exactly, from the shell to the known suffix at the end of the line, with only preamble clauses from an allowlist and one eval word between (the full shape is in [../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md](../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md), Claude Code adapter). A line that does not match keeps its text (with I-23's id replacement) and shows as a mismatch. Amended 2026-10-11 (commit 7d266c9): the suffix's `< /dev/null` is absent when the command carries a heredoc, which is the command's stdin (measured on a project session of 2.1.286 on 2026-10-11, where the strict suffix had made an honest heredoc command a mismatch).
Consequences: A new harness version that changes the wrapper makes every Bash claim a mismatch until the allowlist is extended, which is what D6 asks for. The preamble allowlist is the version-specific part. The payload itself is not inspected: what the claimed command does is still explained by the claim.
Alternatives rejected: Checking only that the line ends with the suffix (a command can still be run before the source, and the program is unchecked); canonicalising the whole line before matching (it would rewrite ids inside the claimed command).

### I-27: A search is recognised only from the harness's install directory
Status: Accepted, implemented (`embeddedRipgrep` in `claudecode/shell.go`). Narrows I-25. Amended 2026-11-02 by I-107: with the capture's record of the agent's program, a search is a run of that program wherever it is installed; this check stays for captures without it.
Date: 2026-10-08 (a security review of the adapter, local only).
Context: I-25 recognised a search by a program path ending in `claude/versions/<digits>` with `--no-config`. Any executable at such a path qualified, including one in the workspace or in `/tmp`, so a harness could claim a Grep and run a script of its own, with the script's subtree explained by the claim (**Verified**: `TestNormalizeRecognisesSearchesOnlyFromTheInstallDirectory`, the workspace and `/tmp` cases, failed before the change).
Decision: The program must be `/home/<user>/.local/share/claude/versions/<major.minor.patch>` or the same under `/root`, the layout of the native installer, which is where the three captures saw it. Everything else keeps its text and shows as an unexplained command or a mismatch.
Consequences: A binary placed inside the install directory is still recognised, which is the same trust as the harness binary itself. That trust extends AS-3 (trusted host) to the installed harness; the overview's AS-3 names only the kernel and the probes. Another install layout (npm, a different `XDG_DATA_HOME`) is not recognised until it is measured and added; its searches then read as unexplained commands, which is loud rather than silent.
Alternatives rejected: Taking the path from the baseline's start-up listing rules (the adapter has no access to the baseline in `Normalize`, and the two would have to be kept in step); checking the arguments' shape (a spoofed binary ignores its arguments).

### I-28: The control task's own command is excluded from the baseline
Status: Accepted, implemented (`CaptureOptions.Exclude`, `Baseline.Excluded`, `baseline --exclude`, `claudecode.ControlCommand`). Extended by I-88 (2026-11-01): a profile's controls may also edit a file, excluded the same way, and an exclusion is matched against the workspace-templated target too.
Date: 2026-10-08 (a security review of the baseline, local only).
Context: A control run must make one Bash call so the harness performs its first-call setup, and the capture script used a no-op marker command for it. Every control run performed it, so it became a rule in every baseline. A rule explains a command's whole subtree without a claim (V7), so any later command with that normalized target was explained, whatever ran beneath it. Before I-26 that was reachable with one appended command; after I-26 the wrapper itself has to be genuine, but the snapshot it sources and the environment it inherits are not observed, so the rule still explained a subtree on trust. The script's comment that the rule "hides nothing" was wrong (**Verified**: `TestPairedControlCommandIsNotABaselineRule` failed before the change).
Decision: `Capture` takes a list of targets that never become a rule or an unstable entry, the baseline file records them, `cmd/baseline` exposes the list as a repeatable `--exclude`, and the capture script passes its control command. The fixture tests exclude it the same way.
Consequences: A later command with the control's target needs a claim like any other. The exclusion is exact and names the command, so it cannot hide anything else. A control task that ran something other than the marker still has to be excluded by hand.
Alternatives rejected: Excluding every level-1 exec from the baseline (the harness's own commands, the snapshot and the git probes, are exactly what the baseline is for); a control prompt that uses no Bash (the first-call setup would then be missing and read as omissions).

### I-29: The harness's own file paths are canonicalised on the observed side
Status: Accepted, implemented (`canonicalFileIDs` in `claudecode/shell.go`, the lock temporary in `tmpName`, `MangledWorkspacePlaceholder` in `pkg/agent/baseline.go`). Amended by I-36 (2026-10-12): file claims' targets are canonicalised too. Amended by I-41 (2026-10-12): the ids are replaced only where runs are compared (`Canonical`), and claims and events pair on their exact paths. Amended by V-38 (2026-10-15): the shell snapshot's file name `snapshot-bash-<id>.sh` joins the families, since a baseline now records what the snapshot command writes beneath it. Amended by I-58 (2026-10-17): the harness's socket `/tmp/cc-socks/<pid>.sock` joins the families. Amended by I-70 (2026-10-27): the mangled workspace turns every character but an ASCII letter or digit into a dash.
Date: 2026-10-09 (audit item 5, step 4, of a security review on 2026-10-08, local only; first capture under P-17).
Context: With every filesystem marked (P-17), a Claude Code startup shows level-0 file events outside the workspace: CA certificates, libraries, its configuration, and files it writes under `~/.claude`, `~/.local/state/claude` and `/tmp/claude-<uid>`. Most are the same in every run and become baseline rules as they are. Some families carry a per-run id (a pid, a session id, a timestamp, a random suffix) and would never agree between control runs (**Verified**: 43 unstable entries over three startups of 2.1.286, all of such families; the first full sessions on 2026-10-09 showed more). The families are listed in [../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md](../architecture/30_agent_adapters.md) (`Normalize`, file events).
Decision: `Normalize` replaces those ids in file events with fixed tokens, as I-23 does in commands; the atomic-write fold also accepts the version lock's eight-hex temporary, before and after its suffix is rewritten; and the baseline names the directory derived from the workspace path by `{workspace-}`, expanded by `Predicate` like `{workspace}`. Claims are left alone.
Consequences: The harness's own writes under its configuration directory become rules by path, so a write there with any content is explained, as it was for the snapshot command; that is the harness's own directory and the same trust as its binary (I-27). A claim that names one of these paths mismatches, which no honest tool call produces. The list is version-specific and is checked against each capture; an id family not listed shows as unstable in the baseline and as unexplained in a run.
Alternatives rejected: A pattern language in the baseline (a wildcard explains whatever an attacker makes it match, V-19); excluding `~/.claude` from the capture (the harness's own activity is what the baseline measures).

### I-30: The Claude Code adapter declares the harness's own directories
Status: Accepted, implemented (`harnessOwned`, `IsHarnessOwned` in `claudecode/claudecode.go`). Amended by I-58 (2026-10-17): the harness's socket `/tmp/cc-socks/<pid>.sock` is declared, not its directory.
Date: 2026-10-09 (with V-24).
Context: V-24 needs to know where the harness keeps its own files. The captures of 2.1.286 show them under `~/.claude/` and `~/.claude.json` (configuration, sessions, transcripts, backups, shell snapshots), `~/.local/state/claude/` (version locks), `~/.cache/claude-cli-nodejs/` (MCP logs) and `/tmp/claude-<uid>/` (task outputs, the fswatch probe), plus the cwd files `/tmp/claude-<id>-cwd`.
Decision: `IsHarnessOwned` matches exactly those prefixes, for a home directory under `/home` or `/root`. It leaves out the install directory (a write to the binary is a finding), the workspace's own `.claude` directory (the project's, which the agent edits), the shell startup files and `~/.ssh`.
Consequences: The list is version-specific like the id families of I-29 and is checked against each capture. Declaring where the harness keeps files is not declaring what it does there: a file event under these directories that nothing explains is still reported, and the baseline still has to explain the regular activity.
Alternatives rejected: Deriving the directories from the baseline's own rules (a baseline is optional, and the declaration must hold without one); taking `HOME` from the environment (the agent's home is not the verifier's).

### I-31: An Edit claims the file's content before and after, from its result record
Status: Accepted, implemented (`editResult` in `claudecode.go`, the fixture reducer keeps the fields). Decides O-17 (whether replaying a partial edit is ever worth doing), removed from open.md on 2026-10-11. Supersedes the Edit-without-hash part of I-22 and outdates part of I-20's Context. Gate passed on 2026-10-11: on the `edits` task captured with Claude Code 2.1.296 and P-20 in the probe (`testdata/paired-2.1.296-edits`), all twelve claims are corroborated, the five Edits on both their input and output hash (`TestProjectEditsClaimTheirContentAndAreCorroborated`). An earlier capture of the same task on 2.1.286 had corroborated the output hashes, six of six.
Date: 2026-10-09 (audit item 6 of a security review on 2026-10-08, local only).
Context: An Edit claim carried no content, and the adapter declared the field inexpressible, so a file could end with any content under a FAITHFUL verdict (**Verified** on the paired fixture). The transcript's result record for an Edit holds `originalFile`, the applied `oldString` and `newString`, and `replaceAll`, so the file after the edit is computable: the first occurrence replaced, or every occurrence.
Decision: When a single result record carries those fields, the Edit's open claims the hash of the file before and its write the hash of the file after; otherwise both stay nil and the verifier reads them permissively. The gate was fixed before any capture: on a capture whose transcript kept the fields, every Edit claim must be corroborated with no content diff, and one mismatch would mean the harness's replacement is not a plain string replace and the claims are withdrawn.
Consequences: Substitution of an Edit's content is a Mismatched finding like a Write's, and an Edit applied to a file other than the one the transcript shows is a mismatch on the input hash. The claims rest on what the harness reports it applied, which is what an honest transcript contains; a transcript that lies about the replacement produces a hash that disagrees with the observed content. An input hash the probe could not settle is nil on the observed side and permissive.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming only the input hash (verifies the premise, not the result); replaying `structuredPatch` (the same information in a harder form); leaving O-17 open (the most used write tool outside P3).

### I-32: The atomic-write fold knows the read-after-rename shape, and harness events do not break a run of opens
Status: Accepted, implemented (`foldRenamedFirst`, `collapseOpens` in `claudecode/stream.go`). Amended by I-39 (2026-10-12): an open of the directory ahead of both the create and the rename is the read of the replaced file and becomes an open of the target, not part of the folded write. Amended by V-49 (2026-10-27): the verifier folds again a run that only events the baseline explains broke.
Date: 2026-10-11 (the capture for I-31's gate).
Context: With every filesystem marked (P-17) the probe reads larger batches later. On the first capture of the `edits` task a Write and three of five Edits were read after their rename: the temporary's handle already resolved to the final name, and the directory's create and rename arrived merged ahead of the file's open, write and close. The fold knew only the temporary-name shape, so each of those writes stayed as raw events and shifted the alignment of an honest run into false findings. The harness also read its plugin list between an Edit's two pre-check opens, which broke their collapse and left one open unrecorded.
Decision: A second fold shape: for one pid, the directory's create and rename (an open of the directory allowed among them), immediately followed by open, write and close of a file in that directory, fold to one write with the close's hash. A direct write with no directory events around it is left alone. In the collapse of opens, an event on the harness's own files (I-30) does not break a run.
Consequences: Both shapes of one write compare equal to the claim. The fold now relies on fanotify reporting the directory records of a merged notification as ambiguous directory-level events. A harness event between two tool opens of a file no longer splits them, which is right for the claim side, which never saw it.
Alternatives rejected: Reading batches sooner to see the temporary (the timing is the kernel's and the load's); claiming the raw events (the claim is the tool call, I-22).

### I-33: The atomic-write fold takes a rename recorded as two in captures made before P-18
Status: Accepted, implemented (`splitRenameHalf` in `claudecode/stream.go`).
Date: 2026-10-12 (the P-17 capture of 2.1.286, **Verified** on its six runs).
Context: On the P-17 capture an honest task read NOT FAITHFUL at an agreement of three controls in five or more, on two renames of the home directory. The harness rewrites `~/.claude.json` through a temporary and a rename, which the probe reports on the home directory as ambiguous events. In the task two of its four rewrites, and in two of the five controls one, hold two renames of the home directory by the agent's process as consecutive events, 50 to 300 microseconds apart, after an otherwise complete replace. The fold took the first; the second stayed as a bare `file_rename` of the home directory, a rule only two controls supported. The same pairs appear after rewrites of the harness's files under `~/.claude`, where they were set aside as harness activity. The capture was recorded on 2026-10-09 shortly before P-18, whose probe drops a `file_rename` that repeats the previous one on the same path by the same pid as the kernel's unmerged half of one operation; a capture made with the current probe holds one rename there. The pairs in the fixture are those unmerged halves.
Decision: When the fold of the temporary-name shape takes a rename that names the directory and is ambiguous, it also takes the next event of the same process when that is another ambiguous rename of the same directory and no event of any process on the directory falls between, and nothing further. The condition is narrower than P-18's, so it applies only to captures recorded before P-18 and keeps them reading as current ones. The home directory stays outside the harness's own paths (I-30, V-24).
Consequences: On the P-17 capture the task is FAITHFUL up to an agreement of four in five and INCONCLUSIVE at full agreement, on a write under `~/.claude` that not every control made; never NOT FAITHFUL. An unclaimed write by the agent to another file in the home directory, direct or atomic, is still an omission. On a current capture the fold adds no blind spot; on an old one it hides what P-18 hides, two real renames of one directory by one process with nothing between read as one. A split rename in the read-after-rename shape (I-32) has not been seen and is not folded.
Alternatives rejected: Declaring the home directory harness-owned (any unexplained rename or create there, by the agent too, would read as harness activity and lose NOT FAITHFUL); declaring only the `.claude.json` family (already declared by I-30; the bare rename names the directory, not the file); restricting the extra rename to replaces of `~/.claude.json` (the split followed any replace in old captures, so an agent's Write could show it too); regenerating the fixture with the current probe (fixtures are not regenerated, and other old captures would keep the split).
See also P-18 in [probes.md](probes.md), which removes the split at emission.

### I-34: The atomic-write fold takes writes that name the target just before a close of the target
Status: Accepted, implemented (`foldAtomicWrites` in `claudecode/stream.go`).
Date: 2026-10-12 (the `tools` capture of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on its task run).
Context: On the `tools` capture an honest task read NOT FAITHFUL. The first Edit of a file produced the directory's create and the open of `<path>.tmp.<pid>.<12 hex>` in one batch, then the write and the close in a later batch, ahead of the directory's rename. That batch was read after the rename, so the write and the close name the final path. The fold of the temporary-name shape (I-22) took the create, the open, the close (which may name the target) and the rename, but not the write, which it accepted only under the temporary's name. The write stayed as a bare `file_write` of the target without a hash, and the alignment paired the Edit's hashed write claim with it (an unverified output hash) and left the folded write unrecorded.
Decision: In the temporary-name shape, a write that names the target belongs to the replace when it is one of the process's last events before a close that also names the target: no other event of that process between them. A write of the target followed by any other event of the process, or followed by a close that still names the temporary, is left alone.
Consequences: The first Edit folds into one write with the close's hash and pairs with its claim. A write of the target by the same process through another handle, issued between the open of the temporary and its close with nothing else of the process between, would be taken into the replace; the folded write still carries the close's hash, which is the file's final content, so a substitution of the final content is still a mismatch.
Alternatives rejected: Taking every write of the target between the open and the close or rename (it would hide a direct write made while a replace was in flight); a new shape beside I-22's and I-32's (the events are I-22's shape with one more name resolved late).

### I-35: A Write with no structured result takes its create or update from the result text
Status: Accepted, implemented (`writeKindFromText`, `resultText` in `claudecode/claudecode.go`). Amended by I-68 (2026-10-27): a Write whose result is marked `is_error` claims nothing.
Date: 2026-10-12 (the `tools` capture of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on its task run).
Context: A Write that creates a file does not first open it, so the adapter claims an open only for an update, and reads which one it was from the structured result (`toolUseResult.type`). A subagent's transcript (`<session>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`) carries no structured result. On the `tools` capture a subagent created a file with Write, the adapter assumed an overwrite, and the claimed open was never observed: an honest run read NOT FAITHFUL on an unwitnessed `file_open`. The `tool_result` text is there: the harness's code (2.1.296) writes `File created successfully at: <path>` for a create and `The file <path> has been updated successfully.` for an update, each possibly followed by notes that start with a space.
Decision: When a Write's result record has no structured result, its kind is read from the `tool_result` text: exactly `File created successfully at: ` and the claimed path, then the end of the text or a space, is a create; `The file `, the claimed path and ` has been updated successfully.` at the start is an update. The text of a result marked `is_error`, one naming another path and any other text give no kind, and the Write is assumed to overwrite, as before. A structured result, when present, decides alone.
Consequences: The subagent's Write claims one write, which the capture corroborates. The kind rests on the harness's wording; another wording falls back to the old assumption and its degradation, which is loud on a create (an unwitnessed open). A transcript that lies about a create claims no open, which removes one claim, never adds an explanation for an observed event: an unclaimed open of the file is still unrecorded.
Alternatives rejected: Inferring the kind from the observed side (a claim is what the agent said, not what was seen); treating every subagent Write as a create (wrong for an overwrite, the unrecorded open would be a false finding); reading the main transcript's `Agent` result (it reports tool counts, not kinds).

### I-36: File claims' targets are canonicalised like the observed side
Status: Superseded by I-41 (2026-10-12): claims keep their exact paths, and the ids are replaced only where runs are compared. Kept as taken; implemented in `claimsFor` until I-41. Amended I-29, which left claims alone.
Date: 2026-10-12 (the `tools` capture of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on its task run).
Context: I-29 replaces the per-run ids in the harness's own file paths on the observed side only, on the reading that no honest tool call names such a path. On the `tools` capture the agent read the output file of its background Bash, `/tmp/claude-<uid>/<dir>/<session id>/tasks/<id>.output`, at the path the Bash result reported. The observed open was rewritten to `.../SESSION/tasks/N.output` and the claim kept the real ids, so the honest Read could not pair: Unwitnessed at agreements 0.2 and 0.4, and a target substitution against an unrelated open of `/tmp` at 0.6 and above.
Decision: Every file claim's target goes through `canonicalFileIDs`, the same function `Normalize` applies to file events, so a claim and an event on one harness path compare equal. Process and network claims are not rewritten (I-23 still applies to observed commands only). Together with V-30, which keeps a baseline event on a claimed path for alignment, the Read pairs with the agent's own open.
Consequences: On the capture the Read is corroborated and the claim side carries no finding at any agreement. A claim names a family, not an instance: a Read of one background task's output pairs with an open of another task's output, and a claim on another session's transcript pairs with this session's. The families are all under the harness's own directories (I-30), so the loss is the precision of which harness file, not whether the agent's file was touched. A workspace path is never rewritten unless it happens to match a family's shape, which needs a `.claude`, `/tmp/claude-<uid>` or `.cache/claude-cli-nodejs` component; in that case both sides are rewritten alike.
Alternatives rejected: Leaving claims alone and teaching the verifier the families (the verifier holds no agent knowledge); dropping the id canonicalisation for the task output family (the baseline would never agree on the controls' own opens).

### I-37: A NotebookEdit claims one write of the notebook with the content its result reports
Status: Accepted, implemented (`notebookClaims`, `notebookResult` in `claudecode/claudecode.go`). Amended by I-39 (2026-10-12): a NotebookEdit claims an open and a write, since its read of the notebook is now kept on the observed side in both shapes it was seen in.
Date: 2026-10-12 (the `tools` capture of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on its task run).
Context: `NotebookEdit` had no mapping, so the adapter listed it as an unknown tool and its write of the notebook stayed unrecorded: on the `tools` capture an honest run read NOT FAITHFUL on it. Its structured result holds `notebook_path`, `original_file`, `updated_file`, `edit_mode`, `cell_id`, `new_source`, `old_source` and `error`. On the capture the sha256 of `updated_file` equals the hash of the observed close of the notebook, and that of `original_file` the notebook's content before the edit. At the kernel the call was one read-after-rename replace (I-32): an open, a create and a rename of the directory, then one open, a write and a close of the notebook, in one batch, which the fold already takes as one write. No other open of the notebook was observed, though the harness's code reads the notebook before writing it. The harness's code also shows that `updated_file` is the text before it is converted to the file's line endings and encoding, that a failed call reports its `error` with `is_error` on the result, and that the harness reports empty texts when it did not write the file itself (**Reported**, from the code of 2.1.296).
Decision: A NotebookEdit claims one `file_write` of `notebook_path`. When its structured result names the same path and `updated_file` is not empty, the write claims the hash of `updated_file`; otherwise it claims no content, and `Expresses` says the format did not keep it, as for an Edit (I-31). A result marked `is_error` or with a non-empty `error` claims nothing and is counted in `UnmappedByTool`. `original_file` is not claimed, and no open is claimed. `ToolSearch`, through which the model loads the deferred tool, stays non-effectful.
Consequences: The capture's NotebookEdit is corroborated on its content, and a substitution of the notebook's final content is a mismatch. The shape rests on one capture: a version whose read of the notebook shows apart from the replace would leave an unrecorded open, and a notebook with CRLF line endings or a non-UTF-8 encoding would read as a mismatch on an honest edit; both are loud. A failed call that read the notebook before failing leaves that open unrecorded.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming an open and a write, like an Edit (the open was never observed apart from the replace, so an honest call read unwitnessed); claiming `original_file` as an input hash (no event left after the fold carries one); listing it as non-effectful (it writes the notebook).

### I-38: A subagent's transcript and metadata are an id family
Status: Accepted, implemented (`sessionDirID`, `subagentID` in `claudecode/shell.go`). Extends the families of I-29. Amended by I-41 (2026-10-12): the family is applied only where runs are compared (`Canonical`), so a run's subagent files keep their ids for pairing.
Date: 2026-10-12 (the `tools` capture of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on its task run).
Context: A subagent's turns are written to `~/.claude/projects/<dir>/<session id>/subagents/agent-<id>.jsonl`, with `agent-<id>.meta.json` beside it (D8). The session id is a UUID and the agent id a short base-36 string, both new every run, so no two runs agree on these paths. On the `tools` capture they were 11 of the 16 to 19 level-0 events in the harness's own directories that nothing explained, and would never become baseline rules even once the controls run a subagent. A search of the capture's normalized level-0 targets for other per-run ids found none outside the families already listed: the model catalog under `~/.claude/cache/model-catalog/` carries a UUID that was the same in every run.
Decision: `canonicalFileIDs` replaces the session id in `~/.claude/projects/<dir>/<session id>/subagents` and below with `SESSION`, and the agent id in `agent-<id>.jsonl` and `agent-<id>.meta.json` under it with `N`. The atomic-write fold still sees the metadata's temporary, whose suffix follows the rewritten name.
Consequences: Control runs that start a subagent give rules for these paths, so a run's subagent bookkeeping can be explained by the baseline. All subagents of a run share one canonical name, so a rule cannot say how many there were. The family is anchored under the harness's projects directory, so a file of the same shape elsewhere keeps its name.
Alternatives rejected: Declaring the subagent files explained without a baseline rule (the declaration of I-30 says where, not what); a family for every UUID under `~/.claude` (the model catalog's name is stable, and rewriting it would merge files the controls tell apart).

### I-39: The read that precedes a replace is kept as an open of the target
Status: Accepted, implemented (`foldRenamedFirst` in `claudecode/stream.go`, `notebookClaims` in `claudecode/claudecode.go`). Amends I-32 (the open of the directory it absorbed) and I-37 (no open claimed).
Date: 2026-10-12 (two captures of the `tools` task with Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on their task runs).
Context: Before it overwrites a file with Write, Edit or NotebookEdit, the harness opens the file to read it. On the first `tools` capture the NotebookEdit's replace was one read-after-rename batch (I-32) that began with an ambiguous `file_open` of the workspace directory, then the directory's create and rename, then open, write and close of the notebook, with no named open of the notebook before it; the overwriting Write showed a separate named open of the file before its batch, and no open of the directory in it. On the second capture the two were the other way round: the NotebookEdit opened the notebook twice by name before a replace through the temporary name, and the overwriting Write showed no named open but its batch began with the ambiguous open of the directory. The fold absorbed that open into the write, so the update-Write's claimed open was Unwitnessed on the second capture, and the NotebookEdit, which I-37 claims as a write alone, left its named open Unrecorded. Across both captures and the `edits` fixture the agent's tools made eleven read-after-rename replaces. The ambiguous open of the directory came first in six, each replacing a file that existed (two Edits in the fixture, the NotebookEdit in the first capture, two Edits and the Write in the second); it was absent from the three that created a file, and from two that replaced one (an Edit in the fixture, the Write in the first capture), each of which opened the file by name just before its batch. The probe marks an event ambiguous when only the parent directory's handle resolves ([12_probe_fs.md](../architecture/12_probe_fs.md), `resolveEventPath`); that the opened inode is the one the rename unlinked before the batch was read is the likely reason, **Unverified**.
Decision: When the read-after-rename fold takes a batch whose ambiguous open of the directory, by the same process, comes before both the directory's create and its rename, it emits a `file_open` of the target, with no input hash since the probe measured none for it, ahead of the folded `file_write`. An open of the directory after the create or the rename is absorbed as before. A NotebookEdit claims a `file_open` and a `file_write` of `notebook_path`, like an Edit, with no input hash. The temporary-name shape is unchanged: no ambiguous open of the directory was seen before it.
Consequences: In both captured shapes an overwriting Write's and a NotebookEdit's open and write each pair with an event. An Edit is unaffected where it also read the file by name: the emitted open is adjacent to the named ones and collapses with them, keeping the first open's input hash (the `edits` fixture's I-31 gate still corroborates all twelve claims). An Edit whose only read merged into the batch would pair its stated input hash with an open that carries none, which V-29 makes Unverified and the run INCONCLUSIVE. The harness's own read-after-rename rewrites of its files (`~/.claude.json`, the policy stamp) now also show their read as an open of the file, which control runs measure in the same shape. The reading rests on the order of events in a batch: an ambiguous open of the directory by the agent's process that merely precedes an unrelated replace in that directory would be read as a read of the replaced file.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming the open only when the result says the file existed (a NotebookEdit always edits an existing notebook; the observed side, not the claim, lost the event); dropping the open from Write and Edit claims (an honest shape with a named open would then leave it Unrecorded); taking the ambiguous open as the fsync of the directory, as I-32 described it (it never appeared when the replace created the file, which an fsync would not depend on).

### I-40: A Read of a whole text file claims the content it read
Status: Accepted, implemented (`readFile`, the Read case of `claimsFor` and `Expresses` in `claudecode/claudecode.go`). Approved by the user on 2026-10-12.
Date: 2026-10-12 (two captures of the `tools` task with Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on their task runs).
Context: A Read claimed a bare `file_open`, so it said nothing a probe could check beyond the path, and alignment could pair it with any open of that path. On the second `tools` capture the Read of the background command's output file paired with a later open, by the harness, of the closing Bash's output file, which I-36 canonicalises to the same path: a timing finding on an honest run. A Read's structured result (`toolUseResult`) holds `type` and, for `text`, `file` with `content`, `startLine`, `numLines`, `totalLines` and, when the harness cut the text to its token budget, `truncatedByTokenCap`. On both captures the sha256 of `content` for each Read of a whole text file (start line 1, as many lines as the file) equals the input hash the probe measured on the matching open: the two workspace files and the background command's output file, six of six. The harness's code (2.1.296) shows how it reads: a call without offset or limit decodes the file as UTF-8, drops a leading byte order mark, turns CRLF into LF and drops a final CR; with offset or limit it joins the selected lines, so the text no longer ends as the file does; and for a background task's output file it strips plugin hints from the text (**Reported**, from the code).
Decision: A Read's `file_open` claims `input_hash`, the hash of `file.content`, when its single result record has `type` `text`, `startLine` 1, `numLines` equal to `totalLines`, no `truncatedByTokenCap`, and the call's input has neither `offset` nor `limit` (or both null). Any other Read claims no input hash: a notebook, an image, a PDF, a range, a cut result, a result with no content, or no structured result (a subagent's transcript). `Expresses` states `input_hash` for a Read exactly when its claim carries one, so a missing hash is the format's, never an opt-out.
Consequences: A Read is checked on its content where the probe measured the file (P-22), and a Read whose content differs is a mismatch; where the probe did not measure it, the claim is unverified and the run INCONCLUSIVE (V-29). On the second capture the Read of the background command's output pairs with the agent's own open, whose measured hash equals its content, and the timing finding is gone; the first capture's outcomes do not change. No checked-in fixture is affected: `scripts/make-capture-fixture.py` keeps only the result's `type` for a Read, so their Reads claim no content. An honest Read of a file with CRLF line endings, a byte order mark, a final CR, bytes that are not UTF-8, or of a task output carrying a plugin hint returns text that differs from the file and reads as a mismatch; none was seen in a capture, and each is loud. A Read of a file outside the probe's measured set (a pre-existing file outside the workspace) is INCONCLUSIVE where it was FAITHFUL.
Alternatives rejected: Claiming the hash of a partial read (the probe measures the whole file); undoing the harness's normalisation (CRLF and the byte order mark are lost in the result, so the file's bytes cannot be recovered); leaving Reads without content (alignment stays free to pair a Read with any open of a canonicalised harness path).

### I-41: Claims and events pair on their exact paths; per-run ids are replaced only where runs are compared
Status: Accepted, implemented (`agent.Canonicalizer` and `canonical` in `pkg/agent/agent.go`, used by `Prepare` and `Capture`; `Adapter.Canonical` and `withoutDeletedSuffix` in `claudecode`). Supersedes I-36. Amends I-29, I-38 and V-30.
Date: 2026-10-12 (the two `tools` captures of Claude Code 2.1.296).
Context: I-29 and I-38 replace the per-run ids in the harness's own file paths so that control runs agree on a rule. `Normalize` replaced them in place, so the verifier saw only the canonical path, and I-36 rewrote claims the same way so that a Read of a background task's output file could pair. Both sides then named a family, not a file: a claim on one task's output paired with an open of any task's output, a claim on one session's transcript with this session's, and V-30 kept every member of a family for a claim on one of them, as I-36 recorded. On the second `tools` capture the Read of the background command's output first paired with the harness's later open of the closing Bash's output file, which only I-40's content claim corrected. The ids are needed only to compare runs: a baseline is built from control runs and applied to another run. Three family patterns are also unanchored (`/tasks/<id>.output`, `/fswatch-probe-<id>`, `.lock.tmp.<8 hex>`) and match that shape anywhere, so under I-36 two workspace files `<dir>/tasks/a.output` and `<dir>/tasks/b.output` compared equal on both sides, contrary to what I-36 stated.
Decision: `Normalize` keeps a file event's path with its ids and drops only the kernel's ` (deleted)` suffix, which names the same file. A claim keeps the path its tool call names. An adapter may implement `agent.Canonicalizer`, whose `Canonical` returns the event with its per-run ids replaced; the Claude Code adapter applies `canonicalFileIDs` to file events. It is applied only where runs are compared: `Capture` takes each rule's target from the canonical event, and `Prepare` hands the measured baseline the canonical event. Pairing, the claimed-path tests of V-30 and V-24, the atomic-write fold and the collapse of opens see exact paths. The verifier does not change: its `Baseline` is a predicate, and the cross-run form lives inside it. Commands keep I-23: the ids in a harness command line that is not recovered are still replaced in place. The JSON formats do not change: a baseline holds canonical rules as before, a ground truth file holds what the probe recorded, and existing baselines and fixtures load and verify as before.
Consequences: A claim pairs only with an event on the path it names. A Read of one background task's output file is Unwitnessed against an open of another task's output, and V-30 keeps only events on the exact path a claim names; a harness event on another member of the family is subtracted as a baseline event, explained and unable to pair. Baseline matching still uses the placeholders, so a control's open of its own task output explains the run's open of its own. The fold sees the raw temporaries, and `tmpName` no longer accepts the `.tmp.N` spelling that only `Normalize` produced. Reports print the raw paths (a command's files, the unexplained harness events). **Verified**: on the six checked-in fixtures and the two raw `tools` captures, with baselines rebuilt at agreements 0.2 to 1.0, every outcome and every count is unchanged, and the attack evaluation's figures are unchanged; on both captures the Read of the background command's output file pairs with the open of that exact file.
Security: With exact pairing a claim must name the exact file the run touched. A fabricated claim on a harness path is corroborated only if that exact path was touched in this run, at the price V-30 states (a claimed read of a file the harness opened; a claimed write must still match the content), where under I-36 any member of the family sufficed: any task's output file, any session's transcript. The agent can learn this run's ids from its own transcript, so the check remains that the file was touched, not by whom. No placeholder makes two different files equal in pairing: claims are not rewritten, and the only rewrite of a file event within a run removes the ` (deleted)` annotation and leaves the path. Placeholders remain in three places, none of which pairs files: the baseline predicate, where one rule explains every member of a family (I-38) but explaining never pairs; a workspace path of an unanchored family shape, canonicalised for baseline matching only, which a rule explains only if a control produced the same canonical path; and I-23's ids in harness command lines, where a claim that literally carries the token pairs with any unrecovered line that differs only in those ids, which gains nothing, since a command claim is free text and could carry the raw line. V-24's set-aside also compares exact paths: a harness-owned event on another member of the family than the one a claim names is set aside and cannot pair.
Alternatives rejected: Keeping I-36 (a claim names a family); a canonical target field on `GroundTruthEvent` and `TrajectoryEntry` filled by the adapter (a second target that every rewrite of the target, the fold's included, would have to keep in step, and a change to the JSON formats); teaching the verifier the families (the verifier holds no agent knowledge).

### I-42: MultiEdit stays unmapped while the harness does not offer it
Status: Accepted, implemented (no case for `MultiEdit` in `claimsFor`; `UnknownTools` lists it).
Date: 2026-10-13 (the `tools` captures of Claude Code 2.1.296).
Context: Earlier versions of Claude Code had a `MultiEdit` tool that applied several replacements to one file in one call. The `tools` task asked the model to use it if it was available; in both captures of 2.1.296 the model had no such tool and made two Edits, and in the 2.1.296 binary the name appears only among permission-rule names next to retired tools.
Decision: Leave `MultiEdit` unmapped. A call to it, from a version that offers it again, is counted in `UnknownTools`, and the report warns that an unmapped tool may have acted, so its effects show as unexplained rather than being guessed.
Consequences: A session that uses `MultiEdit` reads NOT FAITHFUL or INCONCLUSIVE on its writes until a capture of a version that offers it shows its kernel shape and result fields; then a mapping is decided from that evidence, as for NotebookEdit (I-37, I-39).
Alternatives rejected: Mapping it like an Edit from older documentation (no capture shows its result fields or kernel shape on a supported version).

### I-43: An ambiguous open of `/tmp` that the same process's ambiguous delete of `/tmp` follows in one batch is the harness's read of its cwd file
Status: Accepted, implemented (`foldCwdFileRead` in `claudecode/stream.go`).
Date: 2026-10-13 (the two `tools` captures of Claude Code 2.1.296, **Verified** on their twelve runs).
Context: The wrapper ends every Bash command with `pwd -P >| /tmp/claude-<id>-cwd` (I-23, I-26). After the command the harness's process reads that file and deletes it. When the probe reads the delete in the same batch as the read, the file no longer resolves: the read arrives as an ambiguous `file_open` of `/tmp` by the agent's process, followed in the batch by its ambiguous `file_delete` of `/tmp`, with at most an ambiguous open and delete of the session's task directory between. Otherwise the read names `/tmp/claude-<id>-cwd` and only the delete is ambiguous. Across the twelve runs of the two captures (the fixture `paired-2.1.296-tools` is the second), the agent's process made 19 ambiguous deletes of `/tmp`; 6 follow a named open of the cwd file, 6 an ambiguous open of `/tmp` two or three nanoseconds earlier in the same batch, and 7 no visible read. The merged shape occurred in each task run and in two controls of each capture. Every ambiguous open of `/tmp` by the agent's process in these captures has that shape, and no other process deleted `/tmp` in them; the wrapper's shell, whose create of the cwd file can merge too, follows its ambiguous open of `/tmp` with a write and a close. `/tmp` is not one of the harness's own directories (I-30), so above two controls in five the ambiguous open was an unrecorded finding on the honest task run, and at one or two in five a rule built from the controls' ambiguous opens explained any ambiguous open of `/tmp` by the agent's process.
Decision: `NormalizeStream`, after the atomic-write folds and before the collapse of opens, rewrites an ambiguous `file_open` of exactly `/tmp` whose process's next event on `/tmp` is an ambiguous `file_delete` of `/tmp` in the same batch into a `file_open` of `/tmp/claude-N-cwd` that is not ambiguous. Events of other processes, and of the same process on other paths, may lie between. Two events are in one batch when they are less than a microsecond apart: the probe stamps a batch with one clock reading plus one nanosecond per record (P-18), and on the checked-in fixtures and both raw captures consecutive file records of one batch are at most 33 ns apart and those of two batches at least 17 microseconds. The delete is kept. The target is the canonical form of the cwd file family: `canonicalFileIDs` leaves it as it is, so a rule measured on a control that read the file by name explains it, and `harnessOwned` matches it, so an unexplained one is harness activity (V-24). The harness names its cwd files with lower-case base-36 ids, so no file it writes carries the upper-case token, and a claim pairs with the rewritten open only by naming that literal path. The rule does not require the agent's root process: `StreamNormalizer` receives the events without the root pid, and a descendant's file events are a command's content, not aligned positions, so a rewrite there changes no verdict.
Consequences: On the fixture the honest `tools` task is INCONCLUSIVE at every agreement from one to five controls in five, with all sixteen claims corroborated, no finding, and 4, 6, 7, 10 and 10 unexplained events in the harness's own directories; the first capture's task is INCONCLUSIVE at every agreement too, on 16, 18, 19, 19 and 19. The other fixtures and the attack evaluation's figures are unchanged. A named open of a file in `/tmp`, an ambiguous open of `/tmp` that no delete of `/tmp` follows in its batch or that another event of its process on `/tmp` precedes, and every write keep their targets; since no rule for an open of `/tmp` remains on these captures, an ambiguous open by the agent's process is unrecorded at every agreement. The rewritten open no longer corroborates a claim on any file in `/tmp` through the matcher's directory fallback. What the fold hides: the agent's process opening a file in `/tmp` that it then deletes, in one batch, reads as the harness's read of its cwd file, and the delete is explained by the rule for the harness's own deletes. Claude Code's file tools do not delete, so at level 0 only the harness itself produces that shape. A fabricated claim on the literal path `/tmp/claude-N-cwd` would pair with the rewritten open, which corroborates a read of a file that does not exist.
Alternatives rejected: Declaring `/tmp` harness-owned (any unexplained event there, the agent's too, would read as harness activity); accepting the finding because the capture script's default agreement is 1/N (the rule that explains it there explains any ambiguous open of `/tmp`); a target outside the cwd file family (a control with the named read and one with the merged read would not agree on one rule); keeping the event ambiguous (it would cover claims on files in `/tmp/claude-N-cwd/`); requiring the task directory's events in the batch (the first capture's task run has the merged read and delete without them); passing the root pid to `NormalizeStream` (an interface change outside the adapter, for no change in any position).

### I-44: The version lock's rewrite read between its create and its rename folds like its other shapes
Status: Accepted, implemented (`foldNamedLockRewrite`, `versionLock` in `claudecode/stream.go`).
Date: 2026-10-13 (the two `tools` captures of Claude Code 2.1.296 and the checked-in fixtures, **Verified** on 24 runs).
Context: At start-up the harness replaces its version lock `~/.local/state/claude/locks/<version>.lock` through a temporary `<version>.lock.tmp.<8 hex>` and a rename, then reads the lock back. Every run that records the home state directory does it once: the twelve runs of the two `tools` captures, the six of `paired-2.1.286-p17`, the three of `paired-2.1.286-project-heredoc` and the three of `paired-2.1.296-edits`, 24 of 24 (the other 2.1.286 fixtures predate P-17 and record nothing there). The probe reads it in three shapes, depending on when its batches are read relative to the rename. In 11 runs it is the renamed-first shape of I-32: the directory's create and rename, then open, write and close naming the lock. In 8 it is the temporary shape of I-22 and I-34. In 5 it is a third shape that no fold took: the directory's create and the temporary's open and write in one batch, already naming the lock, then either a second open and write of the lock, the close and the directory's rename in one batch (3 runs, the task run of the second `tools` capture, which is the fixture `paired-2.1.296-tools`, among them), or the close in the first batch and the rename in the next (2 runs). On the fixture three controls show the renamed-first shape, two the temporary shape and the task the third, so the task's create, close and rename matched no rule at any agreement: three of its four unexplained harness events at 1/5. The second open is the harness reading the lock back: in the temporary shape that read arrives as a named open after the rename (the fixture's two such controls), and in the renamed-first shape it merges into the lock's own open.
Decision: `NormalizeStream` folds the third shape for the version lock only. An open of `~/.local/state/claude/locks/<name>.lock`, for a home under `/home` or `/root`, whose process's previous event is an ambiguous `file_write` of the locks directory (the create) in the same batch (less than a microsecond earlier, I-43), is followed by events of that process that must each be an open of the lock, a write of the lock before its close, the close, or an ambiguous rename of the directory, until both the close and the rename are seen. The span becomes one `file_write` of the lock with the close's hash, followed, when the span held a second open, by one `file_open` of the lock, which is what the temporary shape folds to. Any other event of the process inside the span, or a missing close or rename, leaves every event as it was. Other files in this shape are not folded.
Consequences: On the fixture every run's start-up rewrite now reads as one write of the lock, followed by an open in the task and the two temporary-shape controls. The honest `tools` task stays INCONCLUSIVE, with all sixteen claims corroborated and no finding, on 1, 3, 4, 6 and 6 unexplained events in the harness's own directories at agreements of one to five controls in five (4, 6, 7, 10 and 10 before).
